## 278. Maraká’nà as Method: Relational Ethics and Indigenous-to-(More-Than-)Indigenous Research

- Number: 104
- Authors: Alexandre Mendes Cursino
- Affiliations: Western University
- Presenter: Alexandre Mendes Cursino
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/93f82e06-3b27-4936-a639-3dd3f99127e0

This paper explores the complexities of Indigenous-to-(More-Than-)Indigenous research through my work with the Maraká’nà community in Brazil. It examines how Indigenous people reclaim their identity through urban land-based learning and reshape their relationship with the Tijuca Forest and its more-than-Indigenous relations. The concept of "more-than-Indigenous beings" critiques colonial and anthropocentric frameworks, emphasizing interconnectedness and advancing relational ontologies rooted in Indigenous epistemologies. The people revitalize Indigenous knowledge and build solidarities within and beyond their community. "Maraká’nà" embodies layered meanings: the collective sound of traditional rattles in Tupi-Guarani, and the native Maraká’nà parrots. Situated near the Maracanã stadium, the community is deeply tied to the Maracãna River and Tijuca Forest. My land-based ethnographic research begins with the river’s pristine waters in the forest, tracing the pollution it suffers as it reaches the community. This contrast lays the foundation for "Maraká’nà as Method," a multispecies, collaborative approach addressing environmental degradation and Indigenous repossession.

---

A partir du concept de la race frontière, nous essayerons d'analyser les interpénétrations culturelles et biologiques des populations sahélo-sahariennes. Or, plus que tout autre époque, la modernité a accéléré les échanges économiques et la circulation des personnes. ## 280. Marginal livelihoods under communism today: Views from northern Vietnam

- Number: 132
- Authors: Paul Lutard; Jean Michaud; Sarah Turner; Mélie Monnerat; Patrick Slack
- Affiliations: Université Laval; McGill University
- Presenter: Paul Lutard; Jean Michaud; Sarah Turner; Mélie Monnerat; Patrick Slack
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/f8009630-9f40-40c6-a49c-6774d48c99f3

This panel explores dynamic intersections in northwestern Vietnam, where the lives of upland ethnic minority communities are being reshaped by global economic trends, ecological challenges, and the pervasive influence of an authoritarian Communist government. Driven by a modernist vision and a neoliberal economic framework, the Vietnamese state is advancing political projects that profoundly affect the livelihoods of these marginal communities. Amid this fragile and evolving landscape, our panel seeks to highlight a source of hope by showcasing the resilience strategies many in these settings have developed. Individual papers will focus on: the complexities of making a livelihood while growing cinnamon (cassia) as a cash crop, and the actors involved; the mindful ways that ethnic minority communities work with new building materials while maintaining customary norms; the impacts of forest conservation policies on local livelihood options; and the positive and negative implications of state policies for upland livelihood opportunities over time.

## 281. « No debemos salvar la lengua, sino a los hablantes » : le cas de la sauvegarde du maya à travers la musique au Yucatán et au Quintana Roo.

- Number: 134
- Authors: Honorine GUICHARD
- Affiliations: Université Laval
- Presenter: Honorine GUICHARD
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/02ab30d6-269a-43c2-b482-5a9ece54b05d

La langue maya yucatèque est une langue de la péninsule du Yucatán. Considérée en danger par de nombreux spécialistes, elle est le cœur de ma recherche de maîtrise. Malgré l’affaiblissement de la langue, on observe, depuis quelques années, un retour progressif des jeunes vers la langue. Marlene Chuc Maldonado considère que la musique en maya intéresse les jeunes jusqu’à leur donner envie de le pratiquer. La revitalisation des langues autochtones est un sujet actuel qui, en anthropologie langagière est relativement étudié. Cependant, l’impact de la musique dans la revitalisation langagière ne l’est pas. C’est là tout l’intérêt de ma recherche : mettre en avant l’utilisation de la musique auprès des jeunes dans la revitalisation d’une langue. 
Mon analyse se base sur un séjour de terrain dans les états du Yucatán et du Quintana Roo dans lesquels j’ai colligé 28 entretiens.

## 282. The Importance of Belief for Female Footballers

- Number: 136
- Authors: Jonathan Miles-Watson
- Affiliations: Durham University
- Presenter: Jonathan Miles-Watson
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/8d8ad3e6-645b-4d3a-8cb1-572719260c8e

This paper applies concepts of belief developed in the Anthropology of Religion, from Gellner (1977) to Luhrmann (2022), to the realm of female football (soccer). It examines the role of belief in success across distinct levels of the English football pyramid and at various stages of player development, focusing on the varied emphasis placed on belief in different contexts.
Incorporating insights from professional players, social players, and my experiences as first a Grassroots and then County FA coach working in the female game, I explore what players mean by terms like 'belief,' 'destiny,' and 'magic'.
Finally, I connect these insights with anthropological theories to propose a new framework for developing future female footballers, that both makes a significant contribution to existing understandings of the psychological corner of the game and acts as a demonstration of the power of applied anthropology to positively impact lives.

## 283. The Anthropologist as Foreign Agent: A discussion of the ethics of fieldwork activism

- Number: 141
- Authors: Cai ap Gwilym
- Affiliations: London School of Economics
- Presenter: Cai ap Gwilym
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/59abf6c5-0dc4-45ee-a717-4a23ea90daaa

This is to be a conversational piece, where I discuss my ongoing fieldwork among progressive political parties in Calgary, AB. This is ethically challenging research for a foreigner to undertake at this time of heightened anxiety around foreign electoral interference. The nature of these anxieties, and of the anxieties of anthropologists who conduct such activist research, shed light upon a number of assumptions we have a tendency to make about the boundedness of field sites, our identities as anthropologists, and even the state itself, and who has the power to influence decision making within it.

---

## 284. Espace de confluence multimodale

- Number: 150
- Authors: Laurence Alain; Vicky Langlois; Zoé Fortier
- Affiliations: Université Laval
- Presenter: Laurence Alain; Vicky Langlois; Zoé Fortier
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/30d17838-be99-4283-8187-4db3543592de

Nous proposons un espace multimodal d’exploration et d’expérimentation visant à rassembler les gens autour de formes alternatives de faire de l’anthropologie, mettant en vedette notamment les zines et le balado. L'espace soutenu par le Laboratoire d'anthropologie multimodale de l'Université Laval rappellerait le caractère spontané et éphémère de la recherche anthropologique, en reproduisant un espace de convergence et de rencontre entre les personnes présentes à la conférence.
D’une part, l’espace serait présent de manière permanente sous forme de station de médiation avec affiches et matériel bilingues présentant les objectifs et les actrices du projet. D’autre part, il serait la scène de deux activités interactives. Ainsi, les gens seraient invités à expérimenter le zine à une station de création spontanée soutenue par l'artiste-chercheure et maîtrisante Zoé Fortier, et à participer à de la production de contenu audio de type balado avec Laurence Alain et Vicky Langlois, créatrices du balado Anthr’potes.

## 285. Contested Spaces: Connection, Commodification, and Shaping Spaces On and Offline

- Number: 153
- Authors: Emma Anderson; Ciara Corrigan; Aleyna Irem Karacan; Rachel Thorn
- Affiliations: York University
- Presenter: Emma Anderson; Ciara Corrigan; Aleyna Irem Karacan; Rachel Thorn
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/8f573c4a-3a13-4ba1-9c4b-be42d4cebe52

This roundtable will explore how various communities are situated in contested spaces, which can be regarded as sites to exercise and promote agency, and where economic and political interests take precedence to form connections, share knowledge, and navigate labour and power dynamics. We address various subjects, including 1) herbalists' medicinal practices and commodification of health shaping medical practices in Istanbul, 2) women’s labour as content creators and how these social media engagements with and within the city of Toronto converge within systems of labour and gender, 3) youth experiences of political becoming and activism in conjunction with emerging gender and sexual identities in the Greater Toronto Area, and 4) how K-pop fans create and find community centred around affective bonds to commodity objects. These perspectives highlight how relationality is vital to conversations of economic and political interests, power structures, and commodification as they influence the experiences of individuals and communities.

## 286. Jonglant entre le développement durable et les évaluations d'impact environnemental : une ethnographie des médiateurs environnementaux

- Number: 159
- Authors: Renata Fontoura
- Affiliations: Université de Montréal
- Presenter: Renata Fontoura
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/bf1d121c-5fd3-47d5-9bdd-7513d2576135

La politique du développement durable est omniprésente dans le monde, voire incontournable. Son discours s’immisce dans les politiques nationales et le lexique des entreprises. La présente discussion propose un angle d’analyse de l’environnement différent de celui véhiculé par les apôtres du développement durable qui en l’envisageant comme une entité monolithique, continuent à ignorer ses autres visions existantes. Je propose de réaliser une généalogie historique de cette doctrine, fruit d'une idéologie occidentale. Cela me permettra de mobiliser une perspective multifacette qui dévoilera les rouages de ses mécanismes. Malgré l'homogénéité des discours et politiques qui la caractérisent, l’appropriation du développement durable par ses propres professionnels n’est pas automatique. Elle se négocie à plusieurs niveaux. A travers une ethnographie menée dans le cadre du travail d’évaluation d’impacts environnementaux, je démontrerai que les professionnels possèdent de l’agentivité. En vernacularisant des savoirs, ils érigent des ponts entre le local et le transnational.

---

## 287. Seabed sensibility: exploring the role of sediments in Marseilles' postindustrial transition

- Number: 167
- Authors: Lukas Ley
- Affiliations: Max Planck Institute for Social Anthropology
- Presenter: Lukas Ley
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/a894de14-1db2-486b-af41-374a6f8567b0

This paper draws on ethnographic research on the perception and management of contaminated sediments in the Bay of Marseilles, France. In Marseilles, coastal development projects usher in a new type of economy in which services and touristic activities prevail (Bartolotti et al. 2021). Housing, entertainment, and recreational activities draw people to the shoreline, where landscapes bear the traces of petrochemical industries and shipping. I consider the seabed as a wavering ecological ruin or “patch” (Tsing et al. 2024), a more-than-human landscape patterned by sedimentary and industrial activity. This landscape exudes poisonous substances that wash up on beaches and complicate coastal engineering. As a morphing ruin, the seabed is a contested object in conversations about long-term liability for oceanic pollution. It gets mobilized for biodiverse and climate-friendly futures in line with postindustrial posturing. I outline “seabed sensibility” as an ethnographic method for understanding the intersections of geology and memory.

---

## 288. Indigenous Knowledge to Build Knowledge: The Case of CESDER and UCIRED in Zautla, Puebla, Mexico

- Number: 168
- Authors: Diana García Barroeta
- Affiliations: Benemérita Universidad Autónoma de Puebla
- Presenter: Diana García Barroeta
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/8d65db7b-64f7-44e0-ad9e-3d90207e9895

This presentation explores how indigenous knowledge shapes higher education through the case of CESDER and UCIRED in Zautla, Puebla. The problem lies in traditional education failure to integrate local epistemologies and practices. Using historical reconstruction, participatory research, and community-based methodologies, we trace CESDER’s emergence as a grassroots initiative driven by Nahua and Totonac communities. The study reveals how local actors, ancestral knowledge, and intercultural dialogue transform education into a tool for autonomy, sustainable development, and social justice. Findings offer a replicable model for inclusive, context-sensitive education.

---

## 289. Killer whales and King salmon: Political paradoxes in North Pacific fisheries management

- Number: 181
- Authors: Danielle Dinovelli-Lang
- Affiliations: Carleton University
- Presenter: Danielle Dinovelli-Lang
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/df124bb8-082a-4cf4-bdd8-72fa8c4b6cc6

Tahlequah has another dead baby. We know this both because she belongs to a group of whales who live in uniquely close proximity to dry land and its hordes of human observers and because she is again carrying the body around on her head, telling us surface dwellers with some intention the bad news. Her news is our news. The story makes headlines. The headlines demand a response. Her gesture, then, is political. And indeed she has already complicated international politics, throwing a wrench into longstanding agreements between the US and Canada over the management of Tahlequah’s favorite food: King salmon. This paper traces the relation between Tahlequah, King salmon, and Alaskan fishing communities.

---

## 290. Karl Polanyi in Retrospect and Prospect: Can the Basic Income Truly Act as a Trigger to Self-Organise a New Resilient System of Energy?

- Number: 182
- Authors: Mu-Jeong Kho
- Affiliations: University College London
- Presenter: Mu-Jeong Kho
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/550338c3-3b96-42ed-b97a-347402f69583

Hitherto, institutional-theory in Marxist-tradition has weakly-connected to radical-theory, particularly of an economic-anthropologist Karl Polanyi, regarding whether basic-income can act-as-a-trigger to self-organise a new resilient-energy-system. This inquiry leads to sub-questions: (1) how the capitalist energy-system is organised-structurated in real-world (objectivity); (2) what the truly-deeper origin-of-crises is; (3) how-whether the basic-income (truly) acts-as-a-trigger self-organisation; (4) if-not, what normative-solutions are, addressing political-landscapes ‘reformism-vs-radicalism.’ This paper, defining self-organisation as institutional-process-of-change-with-struggles of reorganising-reconstituting-restructuring an order-of-out-disorder, aims to critically-reflect on these-questions with the institutional-matrix ‘self-organisation’ structurated: market-versus-non-market; pro-capital-versus-anti-capital, through applying a deeper-understanding on Polanyi into empirical case-studies (with quantitative-data-analysis) on Korea. By doing so, this paper argues: beyond the superficial-issues of market-versus-State, Keynesianism-versus-neoliberalism, there are deeper-issues ‘structuration’ within capitalist systems-of-energy, which most institutional-theory in Marxist-tradition have well-addressed, arguing that an basic-income can act-as-a-trigger. However, these are in-turn valid only when connected to radical-theory of Polanyi critically connecting it into long-term vision beyond such-capitalistic-system.

---

## 291. Beyond Remnants: Rethinking Ruins as Methods in Anthropology

- Number: 185
- Authors: Alexandra Frankel; Luisa Isidro Herrera
- Affiliations: York University
- Presenter: Alexandra Frankel; Luisa Isidro Herrera
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/592ad618-555f-4d1c-b733-d19babc5a656

Anthropology provides nuanced theoretical approaches to ruins as sites of decay and as active participants in contemporary social and political life (Gordillo 2014; Povinelli 2016) that make the spectral tangible (Navaro 2012) and disrupt linear temporalities to shaping the present (Napolitano 2015). 
This roundtable draws on anthropologies of traces, ruins, and debris, to articulate ruins as ethnographic methods. We are particularly interested in how ruins become interlocutors, attune anthropologists to the particularities of their field sites, and inform arts-based and narrative research.  As ruins make claims on imagined and possible futures in different social contexts, this roundtable explores how ruins invoke multiple scales—temporal and material—to mediate between presence, absence, and the work of building livable, thriving futures (Al-Khalili 2024). Ruins as methods will generate discussion on how the endurance of ruins alongside immediate, state, and slow violence, can inform robust de-colonial and ethical ethnographic practice.

---

This inquiry leads to sub-questions: (1) how the capitalist-health-system is organised-structurated in real-world (objectivity); (2) what the truly-deeper origin-of-crises is; (3) how-whether A.I. (truly) acts-as-a-trigger self-organisation; (4) if-not, what normative-solutions are, addressing political-landscapes ‘reformism-vs-radicalism.’ This paper, defining self-organisation as institutional-process-of-change-with-struggles of reorganising-reconstituting-restructuring an order-of-out-disorder, aims to critically-reflect on these-questions with the institutional-matrix ‘self-organisation’ structurated: market-versus-non-market; pro-capital-versus-anti-capital, through applying a deeper-understanding on Veblen into empirical case-studies (with quantitative-data-analysis) on Korea. By doing so, this paper argues: beyond the superficial-issues of market-versus-State, Keynesianism-versus-neoliberalism, there are deeper-issues ‘structuration’ within capitalist systems-of-health, which most institutional-theory in Post-Keynesian (Marx-and-Keynes) tradition have well-addressed, arguing that an A.I. can act-as-a-trigger. However, these are in-turn valid only when connected to radical-theory of Veblen critically connecting it into long-term vision beyond such-capitalistic-system.

## 293. Seabed sediments as material witness

- Number: 191
- Authors: Lukas Ley
- Affiliations: Max Planck Institute for Social Anthropology
- Presenter: Lukas Ley
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/b81c1149-8bd6-443e-acfc-c9f903db9084

In Marseilles, coastal development projects usher in a new type of economy in which services and tourism prevail (Bartolotti et al. 2021). Eco-districts and recreational activities draw people to the shoreline, where former docks, landing sites, and port basins bear the toxic traces of petrochemical industries and shipping. Engaging with degraded coastal ecosystems, scientific and lay examinations of the seabed frame sediments as indicators of water quality and biodiversity. This knowledge turns sediment layers into “material witnesses” of the past, material vehicles of past acts of pollution that trouble the present (Schuppli 2020). I describe moments when contaminated sediments wash up on beaches or prevent dredging activities, requiring infrastructural containment. Attention to the seabed, an elusive object, allows to grapple with the ruins of industrial urbanism ‘beyond land’ and query long-term liability for oceanic pollution. I offer “seabed sensibility” as a method for understanding intersections of marine geology and memory.

---

## 294. In the Land of Giants: Weird Infrastructure in Canada's Oil Sands

- Number: 20
- Authors: Clint Westman
- Affiliations: University of Saskatchewan
- Presenter: Clint Westman
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/59178583-0f4d-4268-b17b-7ec8fa219035

In recent decades, owing to the growth of oil sands extraction in northern Alberta, Canada has moved near the front of the global pack as an oil-exporting nation. Due to increasing production, pipeline bottlenecks had restricted prices in recent years, leading to a marked increase in transport of oil by rail. Currently, with new pipeline capacity to the US and the Pacific Coast, the future of Canada as a major long-term supplier of fossil fuels to the US and Asia-Pacific is increasingly clear. Such infrastructural networks are critical to the sector’s future revenue flow because current production levels will remain stable for decades, continuing to constitute Canada’s principal export. Fossil fuels are thus key to public finances in Canada. Further, Canada’s extraction and processing of oil sands is its most significant source of greenhouse gas emissions (even without considering the end uses of the fuels) and the sector’s emissions are growing, putting climate commitments in jeopardy. Currently, new forms of infrastructure are proposed: in addition to nuclear reactors to power oil sands extraction and refining, these proposals include large-scale carbon sequestration projects, which involve piping carbon dioxide emissions to central locations and injecting them into geological formations. Former Prime Minister Stephen Harper praised oil sands infrastructure as “Brobdingnagian” or gigantic, but I have called it hubristic. In this talk, I want to develop these ideas of hubristic, even weird infrastructure. By weird, I mean such proposals as landscapes with new, cyborg, lakes, massive tailing ponds, untested carbon sequestration systems that in themselves allow for enhanced oil production; use of nuclear power for electricity to tap and refine bitumen: all unsustainable interventions that do not fit with local relations or land-based ontologies.

---

## 295. Neoliberal Knowledge Production and the “Body Unknown”: Describing Cervical Cancer Onto-Epistemologies in Post-Colonial Kenya

- Number: 204
- Authors: Samantha Moore
- Affiliations: University of Manitoba
- Presenter: Samantha Moore
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/c2f8380d-7a18-4cd9-87a5-8de64eb474d2

Africa has long been a site of Western biomedical research wherein colonial public health initiatives sought to govern and surveil African bodies. Contemporary public-health paradigms continue to shape knowledge production in African spaces. Drawing on reflections from a paraethnographic research project in Nairobi, Kenya, this paper examines how neoliberal, public-health ideologies persist within colonial research settings—including how such embedded epistemologies impact anthropological practice for interlocuters, health agencies, and researchers alike. Methodological reflections examining cervical cancer onto-epistemologies among sex workers within Nairobi are presented, with specific emphasis on how oncogenic illness is ‘made known’ in the wake of biomedical paradigms.

---

## 296. Confluences of Transatlantic Spiritualities: The Vodou Flags of Mireille Delice

- Number: 205
- Authors: Pierre Minn; Mireille Delice
- Affiliations: Université de Montréal; Independent Artist
- Presenter: Pierre Minn; Mireille Delice
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/bb445a8e-3e66-4ea4-905a-1f6a99ee0bc3

This proposal is for an exhibit of drapo lwa (spirit flags) created by Haitian artist Mireille Delice. These intricately embroidered banners are created with tens of thousands of glass seeds and depict the spirits and symbols of Haiti's often understood spiritual tradition, Vodou. Having conducted fieldwork in Haiti since the late 1990s, I became interested in Mireille Delice's work as she maintains relationships with family members, employees and clients in Haiti and abroad. This exhibit would highlight Mireille Delice's work working within the conference theme of "Confluences" and present the parameters of an artist-anthropologist collaboration. Mireille Delice would be on-site to present her work and demonstrate her technique (with possibilities of collaborations with First Nations artists who also work with glass seed beads). As an anthropologist, I would be available to translate and facilitate interactions between the artist and CASCA members, and if appropriate, provide ethnographic context for her work.

## 297. From Religious Culture to Quebec Culture: Nationalist Secularism Amid Curriculum Changes

- Number: 219
- Authors: Guilherme Borges
- Affiliations: University of Ottawa
- Presenter: Guilherme Borges
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/55338bb6-d07b-4604-aea2-09c83f58a9c7

Starting in 2024, students in public schools in Quebec no longer have the subject Éthique et culture religieuse in their schedules. Instead, they are now required to take a course titled Culture et citoyenneté Québécoise. The proposed analysis aims to understand the social factors behind this substitution, investigating how it fits into the cultural and political transformations in Quebec. More specifically, this research seeks to observe how this educational change relates to the emergence of what could be called "nationalist secularism", which creates tension with the religious and cultural diversity of the province.

## 298. Social Assistance in the land of Anarcho-Capitalism

- Number: 220
- Authors: Lindsay D
- Affiliations: Dalhousie University
- Presenter: Lindsay D
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/cf26c676-6e4c-4f25-87f3-4e71ac769d3f

Argentina’s Conditional Cash Transfer program, the Asignación Universal por Hijo para Protección Social (AUH), is now well-established.  Fifteen years into its history it covers over four million children and adolescents, about 9% of all Argentines.  Often characterized as a key element of Peronist “assistantielism” the benefit persists as the country’s politics swings from “left” to “right” and back again.  This paper asks how the experience and meaning of social assistance changes in the context of the so-called “anarcho-capitalist” regime of Javier Milei, proposing to cast light on the less-than-obvious relations between policy and politics.

---

## 299. The Ambiguous Agency of a Sessional Anthropologist.

- Number: 226
- Authors: Eva-Marie Kovacs-Kowalke PhD
- Affiliations: UBCO
- Presenter: Eva-Marie Kovacs-Kowalke PhD
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/1e526cea-171c-4d8c-b712-7ddf3506dcaf

The professional trajectory of sessional anthropologists has evolved into a precarious pathway, resulting in a condition of ‘ambiguous agency’ for many in this field. This ambiguity manifests in their capacity to temporarily occupy roles within academia while simultaneously experiencing a lack of visibility within the departments and universities to which they contribute. The contradiction of existing as an invisible body that nonetheless occupies space highlights their invisibility's precarious yet essential nature of being a sessional.

---

## 300. Populism, “Empty Signifiers” and Indigenous Struggle in Canada

- Number: 239
- Authors: Matthew Robertson
- Affiliations: Métis Nation of Ontario
- Presenter: Matthew Robertson
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/992aee84-9e9b-4539-829f-ac77382d149b

This article explores the implications of emergent populist forms of politics and the use of ‘empty signifiers’ for Indigenous resistance movements in Canada. Drawing on the theories of Ernesto Laclau and Chantal Mouffe, it examines how populism offers both advantages and challenges for Indigenous struggle. Canadian populism has the potential to marginalize Indigenous voices, leaving them misrepresented within those movements. These movements often use ‘empty signifiers’—vague terms that unite people—but which could marginalize Indigenous demands for sovereignty and decolonization. Such signifiers can also be strategically employed by Indigenous movements to challenge colonial structures. The article argues that for such movements to succeed, careful collaboration is required between Indigenous and other social groups to ensure Indigenous concerns are prioritized and not overshadowed by broader populist agendas.

---

## 301. The Kampung Spirit of 195 Pearl’s Hill Terrace: Investigating the Impermanence of Heritage Buildings and Artist Communities in Singapore

- Number: 246
- Authors: Samantha Boyd
- Affiliations: York University
- Presenter: Samantha Boyd
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/67c872db-35fc-46e8-b9dd-c390a4352612

This research investigates the sustainability of artist communities within heritage buildings in rapidly urbanizing cities, focusing on the impermanence of "third places" such as art enclaves that serve as informal gathering sites. Based on two months of fieldwork in Singapore, this study draws on Ray Oldenburg’s concept of third places, exploring how the rising costs of living, gentrification, and urban redevelopment challenge the accessibility and availability of these spaces. Using ethnographic methods such as participant observation, The Encounter, and interviews, I examine how these creative communities cope with the loss and transience of their spaces, using tactics of adaptation and resistance. The study engages with urban theorists such as Lefebvre, de Certeau, and Harvey to critically analyze power dynamics, urban change, and affective relationships that form between people and spaces. This study seeks to reframe the concept of community sustainability, questioning whether impermanence itself can foster resilient and dynamic environments.

---

## 302. Strengthening Human Bee Relations: Perspectives in Anthropology

- Number: 255
- Authors: Ursula Bero
- Affiliations: University of Ottawa Alumnus
- Presenter: Ursula Bero
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/8c3d9e35-4099-4ee8-9fd8-0c0f1b7e9289

There are over 800 bee species in Canada, yet we hear about one, the honeybee: Apis mellifera. While honey production is economically important, greater still is the impact of pollination, worldwide. Current bee research focuses on managed honeybee diseases and rarely on the agency of beekeepers who directly manage them. In Anthropology, more attention is being paid to the bee and beekeeper relationship. However, anthropologists often stop short of acknowledging the other bees (and human actors) that contribute to pollination capacity. Not only are there many kinds of bees experiencing significant decline, but there are also many practices in conservation and farming that impact pollination. I argue that approaches which assert a wider ecology of actors are highly salient for better understanding and strengthening the human bee relationship. Both, Multi-species and Social-Ecological Systems perspectives, offer ways to broaden the definition of beekeeping and to further ‘ecologize’ the multi-species body.

---

## 303. Sonic alarms: diabetes technology and protest in the U.S. insulin affordability crisis

- Number: 256
- Authors: Erin Moore
- Affiliations: Ohio State University
- Presenter: Erin Moore
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/eb8c9246-f806-4035-ae79-4a710ea3ad0c

At the protest, everybody’s bloodsugar was high. Adrenaline spikes glucose levels. Activists from several midwestern states had gathered at Eli Lilly’s Indianapolis headquarters to protest lethally high insulin prices. Aged 7-75, many of the protesters had type 1 diabetes, meaning they depend on insulin to live. While activists held signs urging Lilly to “make insulin free” and “release the patent,” wearable diabetes tech alarmed, making sonic demands for more of the insulin Lilly and other pharma companies have made so profitable. From this scene of sickness, biocapitalism, and protest, I consider how insulin affordability activists, a group that congeals at events like the Eli Lilly protest and online, under the hashtags #insulin4all and #diabetesonlinecommunity, endure the U.S. insulin affordability crisis—amid demands that disease self-management be ever-improved, with ever-newer technologies, and pervasive stigma, which tends to materialize at the sight of the technologies that make the otherwise invisible illness visible.

---

## 304. Ethnographic Silences, Temporalities and Water: Centering the fieldwork traces to clarify prevailing nexuses between climate multi-crises in Spain

- Number: 258
- Authors: Elaine McIlwraith
- Affiliations: The University of Western Ontario
- Presenter: Elaine McIlwraith
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/693c4073-fe5a-4c51-9b1b-8c8ac58dcf41

Silences are part and parcel of ethnographic observation, permeating our framings of local ontologies and epistemologies. Despite detailed, holistic anthropological inquiries, gaps in our research arise for numerous reasons. Amongst these, certain clues, connections, materialities or ideas mentioned and their future significance or consequentiality may not be evident until years later. Silences surrounding certain topics or objects may be “never present but ever present” (Napolitano, 2015), left unnarrated by our interlocutors. Within the frame of Tylor’s (1881) and Napolitano’s considerations on “cultural survivals” and traces, I centre an ever-present, yet unaddressed trace in my fieldwork - that of water. Wading through fieldwork engagements with and references to particular traces not only unlocks certain histories. After the fact, it confirms now prevailing futures. It can also clarify past silent connections made, including those that permeate newly-present local responses to global climate multi-crises that determine subsequent future tributaries and resulting confluences.

---

## 305. "Désamorcer la langue pour mieux la partager : Activisme langagier et changements sociaux dans l'est de Belfast"

- Number: 26
- Authors: Vicky Langlois
- Affiliations: Université Laval
- Presenter: Vicky Langlois
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/4fe29a45-f169-44ec-91ee-9367a77336ce

Mots clés: Idéologies langagières ; Identité ; Performance ; Résistances idéologiques ; Revitalisation ; Locutorat ; Aménagement linguistique ; Langues minoritaires ; Réconciliation ; Irlande du Nord
Les Troubles, conflit armé ayant eu lieu entre 1969 et 1998, ont profondément marqué la population et le territoire de l’Irlande du Nord, si bien que les tensions ne se sont pas évanouies avec la signature de l’accord du Vendredi Saint. En 2017, Stormont, l’asssemblée nord-irlandaise, est à nouveau suspendu et l’Irlande du Nord fait face à une impasse politique, notamment en raison des désaccords notables concernant plusieurs enjeux, notamment la reconnaissance du gaélique irlandais. Pourquoi la reconnaissance de l’irlandais faisait-elle polémique alors que d’autres langues celtes sont parlées et reconnues au sein du Royaume-Uni ? Cette question a été le point de départ d’une recherche de terrain s’étant tenue au printemps et été 2020 auprès d’un organisme appelé Turas œuvrant dans l’est de Belfast.
Cette recherche a permis de mettre en lumière un activisme langagier entraînant des transformations idéologiques importantes relatives au sens de la langue, du rapport à la localité et à l’appartenance. Cette présentation propose d’explorer les stratégies de résistances adoptées par Turas et ces bénéficiaires pour transformer le sens associé à la langue irlandaise, jadis outil de différenciation et arme politique, en fait culturel partagé et partageable. Ce faisant, Turas favorise l’introduction de la langue dans de nouveaux espaces et domaines d’usages ainsi que l’émergence d’un nouveau locutorat issu d’un milieu protestant, unioniste et/ou d’ascendance britannique. Finalement, je propose de réfléchir et d’envisager les changements sociaux amorcés et potentiels dans lesquels s’inscrit Turas de même que le rôle des langues dans ceux-ci.

---

## 306. Médiation de la violence et imaginaires militaires

- Number: 263
- Authors: Alexandra Martin
- Affiliations: Concordia University
- Presenter: Alexandra Martin
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/5367127a-3919-44d8-9c80-2c54922d2220

La communication examine les imaginaires militaires dans la culture visuelle étatsunienne contemporaine. Elle analyse différents appareils de médiation de la guerre (jeux vidéos, productions hollywoodienne, simulations militaires) et leur rôle performatif et constitutif au sein des conflits politiques. Pour saisir le régime visuel à l'œuvre dans une ère marquée par une guerre permanente contre le terrorisme, la communication se penche sur l'héritage visuel et discursif des "guerres indiennes" et leur carnavalisation à travers les Wild West Shows du 19e siècle. L’objectif est de démontrer comment ces sites d’un temps révolu façonnent les imaginaires militaires contemporains tel un palimpseste « où les inscriptions des différentes époques se superposent sans s’effacer » (Capot-Rey 1946). Enfin, la communication analyse des cas de « resignification » de mises en scène de la guerre, en montrant comment des contre-discours et contre-archives, issus de la recherche-création, bouleversent les conventions et historicise autrement les conflits violents.

## 307. Collaborative Governance at the Confluence of the Columbia River Treaty Basin and Bellicose Governments

- Number: 277
- Authors: Joanne Taylor
- Affiliations: The University of British Columbia, Okanagan
- Presenter: Joanne Taylor
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/86ade66c-925d-4f8d-bd1b-d0f3f391940e

At the Confluence of the Kootenay and Columbia Rivers in Castlegar, BC, Canada, the Columbia River Treaty governs the waters that both these rivers flow from - the Canadian Rockies.  As the impacts of climate change are felt by communities around the world, in California more specifically and the Okanagan more locally, US and Canadian governments are paying attention to diminishing water supplies in the Columbia River Transboundary Basin. President Trump calls for the 'tap' to be turned on or off to direct water to California. Learning about the Canada - US transboundary Columbia River Treaty negotiations is critical and relevant in a time of bellicose governments and therefore a citizen developed organization is crucial to inform governance bodies. This paper informs basin residents in their efforts to create an International River Basin Organization based on shared TRC and UNDRIP goals for Indigenous reconciliation, and the ongoing Symposium Series findings.

---

## 308. Panel Discussant: Images after the dead

- Number: 278
- Authors: Todd Meyers
- Affiliations: McGill University
- Presenter: Todd Meyers
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/e9e8cde3-23c4-4b60-8590-d0cd2c5cb6fa

What remains of the dead? Traces, fleeting images, feelings. In the funerary portraits of Fayoum from Roman Egypt (1st to 5th century CE; Bailly, 1997), the faces of the deceased confront the living, from a not-so-distant elsewhere, holding us under their gaze. To feel the dead watch us (Ochoa and Gandolfo, 2017) invites us to consider how we experience their presence, and how their presence compels us to respond (Haraway 2016). This panel is an invitation to reflect on ways of seeing the dead, on the images produced in their wake, and on the in/discernability between our worlds. How do we and our interlocutors perceive those beings we cannot face directly even as they reflect our own exposure to their world? What dispositions allow us to perceive the dead? How do encounters with images of the dead inspire responsibility? And what happens when our visions disturb their sleep?

---

## 309. Confluence: Indigenous and western ways of knowing

- Number: 280
- Authors: Corinna Netherton
- Affiliations: Independent
- Presenter: Corinna Netherton
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/df19a2a6-172d-4feb-84dc-213ec487190e

Decolonization must be inclusive of a sustained relationship with the natural world that is a central component of Indigenous ethics and values. Indigeneity can be a baseline to help create a paradigm shift in ideology, offering a value system and red pedagogy as a praxis for sustainable relationships (Grande, 2004). The confluence is applying Indigenous ways of knowing for defending the rights of nature in a colonial system. The right of nature offers a new wave of social and political mobilization to mitigate effects of climate change.
This paper first, discusses the differences of Indigenous ways of knowing and western colonial perspectives. Second, the similarities between Indigenous ways and western perspectives are highlighted as a framework for collaborative and collective efforts to mitigate climate change. Finally, the rights of nature is recognized as a co-management process and as a social paradigm to mitigate to combat climate change.

## 310. Bodies out of place: fitness, health, and (in)visibility in urban Mozambique

- Number: 281
- Authors: Julie Soleil Archambault
- Affiliations: concordia University
- Presenter: Julie Soleil Archambault
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/03228931-ed0b-47e4-844d-ccb6701adc5c

The growing popularity of fitness across urban Mozambique, an activity that many carry out outdoors, brings out some of the challenges women face in their everyday lives regarding competing efforts to control their bodies. In this paper, I focus on the strategies that women have developed to circumvent this control. Locating these dynamics within local poli􀆟cs of visibility and invisibility, I reflect on how shame and self-care work together to produce health-conscious subjectivities.

## 311. “Samba’o na cara da sociedade:” A Quare Linguistic Account of Brazilian Portuguese

- Number: 282
- Authors: Andraya Yearwood
- Affiliations: Columbia University in the City of New York
- Presenter: Andraya Yearwood
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/0f83dedf-6a26-4812-86bd-9165865f9c80

Welcoming a “Quare Linguistic Anthropological approach” (Lane 2019, 10), this study unearths the disparate ways Afro-Brazilian trans* communities do extraordinary things at all levels of language (phonetic, lexical, and discursive) to defy a narrowing of quare futurities. Engaging the reality competition series Drag Race Brasil (S1.Ep1-12; 2023) as a prime site of non-normative linguistics, this work establishes Pajubá within a Black queer—or quare—theorization examining how Black trans*ness is sociolinguistically mediated. This analysis will answer the question: How do Afro-Brazilian trans* communities draw on lived experiences to create new forms of speech or add to the linguistic lexicon to negotiate their identity and place in the world? Such introspection provides a three-pronged analysis of Pajubá to interrogate the role of sentence framing denoting emotion, each chapter centering on endearment, shade, or neutrality, in the Afro-Brazilian Drag Queens’ transmission and transformation of culture.

## 312. (PANEL) Science, crises and uncertainties: confluence of paradigms, practices and actors in current research activities

- Number: 288
- Authors: Geoffroy Carpier
- Affiliations: McGill
- Presenter: Geoffroy Carpier
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/69ed1329-dc2d-4955-949b-2b5359ab9032

This panel seeks anthropological contributions to the study of uncertainties in current scientific activities (e.g. biomedicine, ecology, engineering, space science, agronomy, etc.) In a global context characterized by numerous crises (social, economic, political, methodological) affecting the responsibility and authority of technoscience, a plurality of actors collaborate and compete over the very definition of knowledge production, its social value and the impacts of scientific activities on society at large. These crises often lead individual and institutional actors to work jointly on and at the boundaries of science, and to create new ways of coping with heightened uncertainties, while reflexively reconfiguring their own professional identity, research object and methods of investigation. What particular practical and epistemic confluences are actors bringing to the fore to alleviate the uncertainties associated with current research activities? How are institutions, scientists and other stakeholders reconfiguring the boundaries of science in a context of crisis?

---

## 313. Re-scripting Gender Roles: Performed Masculinities during Recurrent Monsoon Flooding

- Number: 296
- Authors: Ranjita Dilraj
- Affiliations: National University of Singapore
- Presenter: Ranjita Dilraj
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/cb814507-dda6-49a9-9f23-5da98c3952a4

This research explores how repeated monsoon floods reveal deep-rooted socio-structural issues. While flood response may temporarily disrupt traditional gender roles, it can also rescript them in new ways.
Based on ethnographic research, this study highlights how men in the community frame tasks like clearing mud and sludge, and cleaning during disaster recovery as “heavy work,” marking them as masculine and 'unsuitable for women'. This performed masculinity allows them to re-script gender roles, reclaiming tasks that might otherwise be considered women’s work.
Thus, what might look like re-negotiation of traditional gender roles at a glance is a way in which the men in the community re-inscribe them to project their perceived masculinity. This paper thus argues that foregrounding neglected communities can render visible different aspects of community response like the changing dynamics of gender relations.

## 314. Perspectives anthropologiques de la crise du logement au Québec : réflexion sur les causes et solutions possibles

- Number: 298
- Authors: Eric Gagnon Poulin
- Affiliations: GRT Nouvel Habitat
- Presenter: Eric Gagnon Poulin
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/4887e4d9-6721-4053-b4f6-6ebf3ff78992

La crise du logement au Québec résulte de plusieurs facteurs structurels et culturels. D’un point de vue anthropologique, elle s’ancre dans la transformation des dynamiques sociales et économiques. L’urbanisation rapide et la spéculation immobilière ont mené à des hausses des loyers considérables, rendant l’accès au logement difficile pour de nombreux ménages. La gentrification modifie également la composition des quartiers, chassant les populations vulnérables. Les valeurs individualistes pronant l’investissement immobilier comme placement financier exacerbent évidemment cette crise.
Les solutions possibles incluent la mise en place de politiques favorisant le logement social et collectif, la contrôle des hausses de loyer et des pratiques spéculatives, ainsi que la valorisation de modèles d’habitation communautaire. Une approche anthropologique encourage aussi un changement des mentalités, en promouvant le logement comme un droit plutôt qu’une marchandise. Une réflexion sur les causes et les solutions s'impose, notamment en s'inspirant des meilleures pratiques ailleurs dans le monde.

---

## 315. reexamining the idea of "study up" as a conceptual and methodological instrument in anthropology to address the recent political, economic, and environmental crises

- Number: 302
- Authors: Hassan Shahraki
- Affiliations: Carleton University's Department of Sociology and Anthropology
- Presenter: Hassan Shahraki
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/41b61e39-44e6-48b4-b9fe-905e10204727

It has historically been said that anthropology is no longer required to examine power, rules, systems, and structures as a result of the end of colonialism, the end of the Cold War, and the start of globalization. I want to make the case in this roundtable that because of the new events like the start of neo-colonialism with the cooperation of nations like the United States and Israel, like the genocide and ethnic cleansing in Gaza and the U.S.'s proposal to rebuild and occupy it, the rise of the far-right in many nations, including those that were once part of the Eastern Bloc, and the start of traditional devastating wars including the conflict between Russia and Ukraine, the crisis of regular and conventional democracies in many nations, and particularly the climate crisis, at this moment, Laura Neder's concept of "study up" needs to be revised and refocused.

## 316. Bill 84: An Interculturalist Model?

- Number: 303
- Authors: Jérôme Gosselin-Tapp
- Affiliations: Université Laval
- Presenter: Jérôme Gosselin-Tapp
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/e17752be-e411-459c-a991-ea73646ad4a4

In January 2025, the Minister of the French Language, Jean-François Roberge, introduced Bill 84 on national integration. At first glance, the bill incorporates key elements from the conceptual framework associated with interculturalism in Quebec. It references the principle of "reciprocity," a "model of integration," and a distancing from Canadian multiculturalism. However, the bill does not explicitly mention interculturalism or fully engage with the language of interculturalism. This chapter examines Bill 84 in light of theories on Quebec interculturalism to better understand the philosophical framework in which the Coalition Avenir Québec government’s national integration model is situated.

---

## 317. Quebec Interculturalism in English Canadian Media (1980-2024): A Vector of Connection or Differentiation?

- Number: 304
- Authors: François Rocher
- Affiliations: Université d'Ottawa
- Presenter: François Rocher
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/5e71730b-5391-4258-8860-f2b880445319

This presentation offers a discourse analysis of how the term "interculturalism" has been used in seven English-language media outlets in Canada, as well as in the Montreal-based anglophone newspaper The Montreal Gazette, from 1980 to 2024. Outside Quebec, interest in interculturalism emerged during the work of the Consultation Commission on Accommodation Practices Related to Cultural Differences (Bouchard-Taylor Commission) and continued during debates surrounding Quebec’s secularism model. The Montreal Gazette expressed interest in interculturalism earlier when the City of Montreal established its Advisory Committee on Intercultural Relations. This presentation highlights how interculturalism is understood in anglophone Canadian media, the criticisms it faces, and how it aligns (or does not) with Canadian multiculturalism.

## 318. Interculturality in Occupational Health and Safety: Issues, Practices, and Perspectives in a Diverse Quebec Context

- Number: 308
- Authors: Daniel Côté; Jessica Dubé
- Affiliations: IRRST
- Presenter: Daniel Côté; Jessica Dubé
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/a2bdd597-5445-4a52-82b6-c52fcfc40b34

This presentation explores intercultural dynamics in occupational health and safety (OHS), drawing on studies and research projects conducted in Quebec. These efforts have enabled labor unions, community organizations, and employers to develop educational materials and propose concrete measures to improve OHS training, intercultural communication, reintegration support after workplace injuries, and the development of intercultural skills at individual, collective, and organizational levels. Our presentation offers perspectives on the future of intercultural skills development in OHS and calls for stronger collaboration between institutions and affected communities.

## 319. In This Here Place, We Flesh: Belonging and Arts Creation in the site of former Juke Joints.

- Number: 314
- Authors: Shevan Bastianpillai
- Affiliations: independent scholar
- Presenter: Shevan Bastianpillai
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/8245060f-e4ad-4308-82b2-d4c7ed2d355d

The Black southern phenomenon known as juke joints are relics of Black community arts practice.  In encountering the ruins of former juke joints, my research focuses on the use of critical fabulation as a methodology in understanding the power of place making that transformed ordinary households into radical spaces that developed and sustained Black sociality, intimacy and acceptance.  Through his process, Black musicians imbued their creations with meaning that transcended the plantation logics of post slavery, and in doing so, broached an act of love so radical as to transform society.  Juke joints and other spaces comprising the chitlin circuit, disappeared in the late 1980s and early 90s; these ruins of Black belonging rupture the commonplace violent histories and the aftermaths of post slavery life for Black folk in the American South by insisting on an affective reimagining of Black futurity in which belonging begets community as prelude to solidarity.

---

## 320. Towards an Ecological Economic Anthropology

- Number: 318
- Authors: Bobi Steel
- Affiliations: McGill University
- Presenter: Bobi Steel
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/8d1884e8-639c-444f-9d4b-fb8ee871c1f8

Ecological economics and anthropology share concerns at the interface of human social organization with the non-human environment. Many of the core tenets expressed by substantivist anthropologists such as Karl Polanyi are also found in ecological economics textbooks, but rarely do scholars of anthropology directly engage with the work of ecological economists. This panel explores the development of ecological economic anthropology, research at the confluence of ecological economics and anthropology. Bringing together scholars working across/beyond disciplinary boundaries, this panel seeks to advance the integration of ecological perspectives with economic analysis and anthropological inquiry, a research approach offering a holistic lens for understanding dynamic relationships between economic systems, cultural forms, and the ecological boundaries of our planet. By examining case studies from diverse contexts, panel members will highlight innovative frameworks and methodologies they have used to combine qualitative and quantitative approaches in economics and anthropology to better understand human-environment interactions.

---

## 321. “Brother” and “Uncle” revisited: Hy Van Luong on person reference

- Number: 322
- Authors: Luke Fleming
- Affiliations: Université de Montréal
- Presenter: Luke Fleming
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/40cf6cbb-04ba-46a0-99e6-b5a941ea5da0

Hy Van Luong’s work in the 1980s and 90s on the social pragmatics of person reference in Vietnamese was path-breaking. His work moved beyond a view of kin terminology as the semantic encoding of genealogical relations to an understanding of the extension of kin terms as socially consequential, “goal-directed action.” It highlighted the divergent social pragmatic patterning of reference to speech participants in Asian languages, as contrasted with European languages. But it also suggested how broader norms of person reference (e.g., the honorific alternation between kin term and names) may be conditioned by children's slow acquisition of perspective-relative kin terminology. In this paper, I reprise the arguments and themes raised in the writings of this period, and I suggest how they shed light on under-recognized outstanding problems in the sociolinguistic typology of names and kin terms.

---

## 322. Preserving Settler-Colonial Landscapes in Climate Adaptation

- Number: 324
- Authors: Jeremy Trombley
- Affiliations: University of Western Ontario
- Presenter: Jeremy Trombley
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/5754318e-6a6c-4a71-abfb-97779bfceed1

In the Hood River valley of Oregon, the confluence of Farms and Fish has generated an awkward, but stable relationship between Indigenous and white settler communities. Recent efforts, largely funded by Indigenous communities themselves, have made modest advances in restoring an Indigenous landscape that had previously been overwritten with settler-colonial fruit farming supported by extractive irrigation. However, glacier recession due to climate change threatens both the settler-colonial and the Indigenous landscapes creating systematic pressure to adapt within a system of artificial water scarcity. In this presentation, I examine the legacy of settler-colonialism in the Hood River basin and the ways that it is reproduced within emerging discussions about climate adaptation in the watershed. This discourse threatens to undo the uneasy balance of Farms and Fish and re-inscribe settler colonialism on the landscape. I propose that settler discourse must be examined and challenged in climate adaptation discourse.

---

## 323. Chronicling Loss: Reimagined Archives in the Wake of a Disaster

- Number: 326
- Authors: Secil Dagtas
- Affiliations: University of Waterloo
- Presenter: Secil Dagtas
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/2113c858-6547-4ac8-8598-65a2ae08b24b

This paper examines how minority communities in Antakya, Turkey, document the city’s destruction following the 2023 earthquakes, treating it as an archive of state violence. Cities serve as archives, embedding histories in their landscapes, but what happens when all material traces are erased? Antakya—a city shaped by displacement, coexistence, and dispossession—has long been home to Arab Orthodox, Alawi, and Jewish minorities, alongside recent Syrian arrivals. In the aftermath of the earthquakes, these communities, now dispersed across Turkey and beyond, continue to chronicle the city’s ruins through digital records, artistic expressions, and other forms of documentation, asserting their collective presence in anticipation of future challenges. Their archival practices go beyond preservation, reframing loss as a political and temporal intervention. By examining how memory and documentation persist beyond material form, this paper rethinks what it means to chronicle a city’s history when its physical traces have been violently erased.

---

## 324. Psychoanalysis Meets Sociocultural Studies: Unpacking Pedagogical Challenges and Fostering Opportunities

- Number: 336
- Authors: Ahmadreza Mohammadpouryazdi; Marc Lafrance
- Affiliations: PhD Candidiate; Associete Professor
- Presenter: Ahmadreza Mohammadpouryazdi; Marc Lafrance
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/7774bddd-9011-4f87-9b2c-b287a25ecb45

This paper presents a structured theoretical framework for teaching psychoanalysis to undergraduate students in sociology and anthropology. Emphasizing interdisciplinary engagement, it explores both the opportunities and challenges of integrating psychoanalytic thought into social sciences. The study is based on a two-stage literature review: the first examines psychoanalysis' historical and sociocultural intersections, while the second assesses pedagogical challenges and solutions. The paper critically engages with Crapanzano’s insights, linking critiques of classical anthropology and psychoanalysis. It also provides practical strategies, including experiential learning and the application of positionality and countertransference to help students navigate complex concepts. By addressing common stereotypes and misunderstandings, the paper advocates for a more nuanced, interactive approach to psychoanalytic education. Ultimately, it argues that teaching psychoanalysis extends beyond theory, fostering critical thinking, intellectual curiosity, and cultural awareness, equipping students with analytical tools for understanding selfhood, culture, and society.

## 325. Haptic confluences and plastic bodies in ritual performances in Japan

- Number: 342
- Authors: Maria Ibari Ortega
- Affiliations: Australian  National University
- Presenter: Maria Ibari Ortega
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/4f38d116-0c0d-40b5-900e-79e515ffcb5d

In this presentation, I will briefly discuss the ethical and moral implications underpinning “performative plasticity”, a concept I have elaborated throughout my anthropological research of the Hadaka Matsuri of Mitsuke District, Shizuoka Prefecture. I depart from my interpretation of the concept of sensation as plasticity, defined by Tom Sparrow (2015), to analyse its role in fostering resonance and permeability among local parishioners. I argue that performative plasticity is informed by and transmitted by the local specificities of what I call Cultural Hapticity. Here, it is pertinent to frame motion and tactility following the principle of “incipient action” (Manning, 2009). These shall not be reduced to human kinetics or corporeal proximity, for it also includes the emerging motion of Shinto deities, ritual objects, celestial and liquid bodies (the moon; the ocean); as well as the textured touch of atmospheres, and the haptic aurality produced by resonating objects used in different rituals.

---

## 326. Contextualizing archaeological disinformation: A case study from Québec

- Number: 344
- Authors: Julien Riel-Salvatore
- Affiliations: Université de Montréal
- Presenter: Julien Riel-Salvatore
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/e72fc0b7-f4d9-4949-bfe0-a0a7cd175ed0

Due to its fragmentary nature, archeological material is frequently a target of pseudoscientific and fraudulent interpretations that the general public is often ill-equipped to recognize as incorrect. This paper discusses the so-called “Phoenician stones” from Sherbrooke, QC, as an example of how such fanciful interpretation can come to be made and promoted, but especially how and why they can endure even long after they have been debunked. We discuss the history of these peculiar objects and present the historical context in which they were first brought to the attention of scientists and the public. We then present a new analysis of them that confirms prior suggestions from the 1970s that their ‘Phoenician’ attribution is specious. This permits an exploration of how the Phoenician stones’ existence became a zombie claim, promoted by different segments of Québec society quite distinct motivations, tying into recent discussions about the anthropology of misinformation.

---

## 327. The Algorithm of Care: Self-Care, Community, and Capitalism in Digital Spaces

- Number: 345
- Authors: Gwyn Peters
- Affiliations: York University
- Presenter: Gwyn Peters
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/3d632064-c4c9-4d03-b938-8a2572425a7d

As physical third spaces disappear, social media platforms have become key sites for community formation and the commodification of care. These digital spaces blur the line between intimacy and spectacle, shaping how people seek connection, express vulnerability, and experience care in an algorithm-driven environment. Users are encouraged to approach self-care as an individual, consumer-driven responsibility while also seeking connection through commodified digital relationships. Wellness influencers, digital therapists, and self-help content creators cultivate parasocial bonds that transform emotional support into a transactional and performative experience. Digital intimacy functions both as a surrogate for and an extension of traditional social bonds, raising questions about the monetization of care, the labour of emotional engagement, and the accessibility of support in an increasingly digital landscape. By framing social media as a confluence of connection and commerce, this paper considers how digital platforms are reshaping the future of social solidarity and care.

---

## 328. Symbolism and Narrativity at Folkloric Festivals

- Number: 346
- Authors: Blanche Deschênes
- Affiliations: Concordia University
- Presenter: Blanche Deschênes
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/23b68847-1de4-41b4-a987-d7d02123c62c

In the summers of 2023 and 2024, I attended and volunteered at four folkloric festivals in Quebec’s Côte-du-Sud region to examine how local identities are constructed, maintained, and transformed over time. Festivals are spaces and moments where confluence happens, making them prime sites for the narratives of lumberjacks, sailors, and musicians to be both written and rewritten. By reproducing some symbols and rituals while abandoning others, festival organizers and attendees continually reshape how people connect to their territory and community.

## 329. Waiting for Disability Certification: Cripping Experiences of Bureaucratic Time

- Number: 347
- Authors: Kim Fernandes
- Affiliations: University of Toronto
- Presenter: Kim Fernandes
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/730b98ce-1a7a-4d65-b5d8-dffce1aaced3

How is bureaucratic time experienced by disabled people seeking to be seen by the state? And what role does disability expertise play in how disabled people come to shape bureaucratic interactions and, by extension, their relationships with the state? Across India, a person is legally considered disabled only when they have applied for - and received - a paper-based disability certificate, or, more recently, a unique disability ID (UDID) card. However, the wait to receive a disability certificate is significant, and is shaped by significant doubt and mistrust. As a result, although disabled people across India often apply for a certificate, it takes many rounds of interacting with medical-legal bureaucracy and months of waiting before they can receive one. This paper thinks with disabled peoples’ experiences of bureaucratic time in urban India through the lens of crip time to demonstrate how time comes to be cripped through the certification process.

---

## 330. Crip Technoscience and Data Justice: Datafying and Governing the Disabled Body in Urban India

- Number: 348
- Authors: Kim Fernandes
- Affiliations: University of Toronto
- Presenter: Kim Fernandes
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/2827c69a-5f33-4d64-92bd-0303e28b9d2d

My work focuses on the paper-based disability certificate and its successor, the digital unique disability ID (UDID) in India. Amidst a backdrop of widespread mistrust in government statistics about disability in India, I think alongside these two technologies of identification and enumeration - the disability certificate has been widely sought after by individuals and communities in India since the mid 1980s and has recently been replaced by the UDID. My project frames ethnographic conversations about shadiness and trustworthiness within the broader lens of crip data studies, attending to how disabled peoples’ embodied knowledge enables them to actively participate in and shape bureaucratic processes. I am particularly interested in thinking about the circumstances under which disabled people draw upon their embodied experiences to contest decisions by automated systems, such as during moments when the evaluation system comes to calculate a different disability percentage than the disabled person would have anticipated.

---

## 331. Money: An Anthropological Research Journey into Confluence.

- Number: 37
- Authors: Cynthia Tahhan
- Affiliations: university of calgary
- Presenter: Cynthia Tahhan
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/a71136fe-60b5-44bc-8dc9-2520ca04b389

The Anthropology of Money is a study that looks at the cultural aspects of currency and financial and monetary systems. This type of anthropological study is influenced by historical global events such as the establishment of the Federal Reserve, the establishment of the petrodollar the great depression and the 2008 stock market crash. One of the most powerful influences in this study of money includes the willingness of early anthropologist Bronislaw Milanowski to study and catalogue the Kula Ring System, a system of trade and barter of goods across the Trobrian islands. Currently, this anthropological study is an integrative and analytical exercise that considers the cultural and social forces that shape our current financial realities leading to social conflicts and war across temporal and spatial realities.

---

## 332. Empire, islands and health citizenship: Engaging with peripheries to understand evolving care experiences in the United Kingdom

- Number: 39
- Authors: Anna Horton
- Affiliations: Anthropology department, McGill University
- Presenter: Anna Horton
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/af6e68bc-d25d-4ec0-813a-152135b399d4

This presentation explores how anthropologists can engage with confluences of different and overlapping ‘scapes’ to understand evolving forms of health citizenship in increasingly globalized health systems. I do this by exploring the links and legacies that exist between the United Kingdom (UK) and British overseas territory, the British Virgin Islands (BVI), which are connected by healthcare governance, care pathways, and flows of capital - particularly as money moves from UK tax payers to BVI tax havens through controversial public-private partnerships in UK healthcare. I consider how unbounding and de-centering the UK health system to explore its edges and outer realms in the BVI may help us to better understand ways that layered healthcare structures surface in collective imaginations and lived experiences in the UK. Such practices of ‘thinking away’ will help anthropologists to better understand confluences and constellations that shape people’s relationships with health, illness and care in globalized health-scapes.

---

## 333. Walking the Talk: Creation of a Cultural Safety Course by a Medical Regulatory Authority

- Number: 55
- Authors: Nathalie Duchesne; Mauril Gaudreault; Emilie Bertelle; Isabelle Tardif
- Affiliations: Collège des médecins du Québec
- Presenter: Nathalie Duchesne
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/edb220c8-8564-41a1-a192-7cbc12eb4653

This paper narrates the development of a Cultural Safety (CS) course by a Canadian medical regulatory authority, grounded in the principles of Equity, Diversity, Inclusion, Social Justice, and Reconciliation (EDI-R). Acknowledging the Western and patriarchal origins of medicine, the course's development embraced these principles in both its methodology and teaching. The narrative demonstrates how grounded theory, decolonizing methodologies, and anti-oppressive approach can be purposefully used to address systemic discrimination in healthcare.
The process involved qualitative data collected through rapid ethnography methodology within discriminated and marginalized communities, surveys with members of the medical regulatory authority (MRA), and subsequent validation consultations with a variety of stakeholders. The collaborative development of course content also restructured traditional MRA creation and teaching methods, as well as the use of art and multimedia tools to enhance learning.
This narrative exemplifies how leading through example is necessary to create lasting impacts on healthcare delivery.

---

## 334. Extending Healthcare Cultural Safety principles to all type of discriminations: a proposed memory aid for the physicians based on Intersectionality

- Number: 56
- Authors: Nathalie Duchesne; Emilie Bertelle; mauril gaudreault
- Affiliations: Collège des médecins du Québec
- Presenter: Nathalie Duchesne
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/072798c5-acfe-4469-ae7d-a82183c7bbc9

Despite three decades of discussion around cultural safety, it remains in its infancy in healthcare. Meanwhile, the concept of intersectionality has broken the silos of research and analysis of inequality systems by advocating for a comprehensive consideration of all aspects of identity.
In the critical search for improving healthcare for all, we argue that the simultaneity and multiplicativity brought by intersectional considerations enrich the analysis and discourse of cultural safety training and endeavors by better reflecting and addressing the lived reality of people, power relations, and social inequalities encountered in healthcare. Extending the concepts of cultural safety to all types of discrimination, and not only to settler colonialism and racism, we herein present a practical memory aid for the everyday practice of culturally safe healthcare providers. Easily adaptable to the local context of care, it bears the advantage of a uniform practical approach for healthcare providers and all patients’ identities.

## 335. Kayfabe Kisses on the Padded Turnbuckle: Racialized Trans/feminine Professional Wrestlers

- Number: 66
- Authors: Daniel Uy
- Affiliations: University of Toronto
- Presenter: Daniel Uy
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/adc4810e-f963-48b5-b09c-5a1584042773

Professional wrestling has always been an arena where both cultural and political ideologies collide in the ring. The majority of audiences favour representation that has not always uplifted or represented queer identities, let alone racialized queer identities. This has seen new changes with All Elite Wrestling (AEW) showing public support for racialized queer athletics. This presentation highlights how two Black and Indigenous trans wrestlers crafted story-telling, have the power to change social narratives both in the wrestling ring, but in society at large. The aim is to uplift the physical talent of Sonny Kiss and Nyla Rose on their abilities to be professional and authentically who they are. It also highlights key moments of fun and joyful play. In doing this, we will contextualize how constructing these narratives can culturally change thinking and queering the ideas of local heroes in the wake of looming legislation endeavouring to do the opposite.

---

## 336. Anthropologie de chez soi : manière de raconter son quotidien en Afrique

- Number: 76
- Authors: joel Ipara Motema
- Affiliations: Université de Kinshasa
- Presenter: joel Ipara Motema
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/be323944-a69c-4584-a7dc-a4be2c540546

Restée pendant longtemps, une science énigmatique, l’anthropologie se positionne aujourd’hui en tant qu’un savoir dont l’objectivité des faits étudiés, ne peut s’expliquer qu’à travers le seul paramètre géoculturel plutôt que par la relativité socioculturelle. Cette réflexion démontre que l’appropriation et l’interprétation des phénomènes sociaux seront analysés ici comme une manière de raconter le quotidien, d’entrer en contact avec l’Autre, et d’explorer les nouvelles pistes de recherche devant servir de base au nouveau récit pour l’anthropologie contemporaine et le développement.

## 337. Le paysage langagier lors des Jeux olympiques et paralympiques de Paris 2024 Résumé : (150 mots)

- Number: 81
- Authors: Honorine GUICHARD
- Affiliations: Université Laval
- Presenter: Honorine GUICHARD
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/5a6c33a6-6977-4a5e-a7bb-19a3e2729fd1

Durant l’été 2024, les Jeux olympiques et les Jeux paralympiques se sont déroulés à Paris. Je propose de revenir lors du prochain colloque de la CASCA, la place des différentes langues lors des jeux olympiques et paralympiques. Dans un premier temps, j’aborderai les langues officielles des jeux olympiques et paralympiques. En effet, les deux éditions ne répondent pas aux mêmes comités, aux mêmes règles et ont des langues officielles différentes. Ensuite, je passerai à l’analyse des panneaux d’affichage que j’ai photographiés dans les transports en commun, dans les lieux d’épreuves et durant les épreuves. Je terminerai par l’analyse des panneaux d’affichage destinés au personnel, aux volontaires et aux sportifs. Ce sujet résonne particulièrement avec le thème de l’édition 2025 du colloque de la CASCA. En effet, pendant 2 mois, Paris a été la confluence de tous les regards, la confluence du monde sportif et la confluence de tous les espoirs.
