## 338. Art as Ethnography: Navigating Home, Identity, and Belonging in the Pakistani Diaspora of Toronto

- Number: 89
- Authors: Seemil Chaudhry
- Affiliations: york university
- Presenter: Seemil Chaudhry
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/5eb65619-9a5b-4d25-81ff-b84ef94120f5

This paper explores the confluence of art and anthropology to examine how these disciplines intersect to produce new ethnographic knowledge. Focusing on the South Asian—Pakistani—diaspora in Toronto, I investigate how artists within this community use art to navigate and express complex narratives of home, belonging, identity, and politics. By engaging with artistic practices, I highlight how these creators make sense of memories and contested realities, offering insights into shared and divergent experiences of diaspora. This study demonstrates how art is a dynamic space for reflecting on and constructing diasporic identities, contributing to broader anthropological discussions on cultural memory and the politics of belonging. Through this lens, I argue for the potential of art-anthropology collaborations in deepening our understanding of diasporic life and its multifaceted realities, and to consider art as ethnography, which can offer more nuanced, sensory, and experiential knowledge of cultural phenomena (Schneider & Wright, 2014).

---

## 339. Inclusion et éducation au Québec : Le « succès de tous les élèves », un modèle de gestion axé sur l’exclusion.

- Number: 90
- Authors: Roxane Archambault
- Affiliations: Labrri
- Presenter: Roxane Archambault
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/e00313d5-876e-4b06-8469-d3cdd13f67a6

Je propose d'explorer les formes invisibles de travail d'inclusion réalisées par les enseignant.e.s et le personnel scolaire, en analysant leur impact sur leur bien-être personnel et social. Des périodes prolongées de stress peuvent produire de l’épuisement émotionnel, un sentiment d’échec professionnel, une baisse de l’accomplissement personnel et une tendance à la dépersonnalisation, qui signifie: “...Lorsque les enseignants développent des attitudes négatives envers les étudiants, les collègues et les parents.” (Rojo & Minier, 2015). À travers la théorie des ordres de Grégory Bateson, mon travail articule ces différentes dimensions (idéologique, affective, morale, juridique, et pratique) pour modéliser les tensions systémiques qui façonnent l'inclusion dans l'éducation, et en société.

## 340. Colonial Museums and Indigenous Collections as Hosts and Agents in Treaty Diplomacy: Complex Potentialities

- Number: 98
- Authors: Bradley Clements
- Affiliations: University of Toronto
- Presenter: Bradley Clements
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/f987f544-7066-48c7-906d-3c2ab918ca5c

Diplomatic gifts play important roles as agents in Indigenous international law in northeastern Turtle Island. Such agents were notably active in Indigenous-colonial relations of the region through the 17th, 18th, and 19th centuries. However, many of these diplomatic agents have become museumified as neutralized artifacts in anthropological collections. There are recent examples of this anthropological museumification and political neutralization being challenged in and through some Canadian museums. My presentation will discuss three such examples, at the McCord Stewart Museum in Montréal, Québec, the Peterborough Museum and Archives in Peterborough, Ontario, and the Manitoba Museum in Winnipeg, Manitoba. Exhibitions and activations of diplomatic gifts at these three institutions - wampum belts, quilled birchbark boxes, and diplomatic pipes, respectively - may provide evidence of and support for the continued diplomatic agency of such material beings, and for the complicated role of Canadian museums as hosts and actors in these ongoing diplomatic engagements.

---

## 341. Reconsidering Youth Pregnancy in the Willamette Valley: Engaging Adult Support Providers

- Number: 99
- Authors: Olivia Brophy
- Affiliations: University of British Columbia
- Presenter: Olivia Brophy
- Program status: Submitted
- URL: https://event.fourwaves.com/casca2025/abstracts/8d42db1b-08ae-40ea-a7e6-67d8d068c2bd

Youth pregnancy is generally seen as detrimental to the life chances of young parents and their children. In the United States, it is painted as a drain on government resources and a threat to collective social wellbeing. This paper uses medical anthropological perspectives to discuss the ways in which such negative framings are confronted by support providers in Oregon’s Willamette Valley through their work. While negotiating a variety of socio-economic, religious, and political affiliations, these support professionals coalesce around a belief that young people are worthy of care. Understanding the support systems young people draw upon provides important insight for those seeking to address the actual human impact of restrictive reproductive health realities in the United States.
